⏺ I've finished Step 1 and written the report to docs/step-1-research.md. No
  other files were touched, nothing was installed and nothing was deployed. The
  report labels every claim as verified, secondary, unverified or my assumption.

  Recommendation
  - Audience: Czech beginner investors on mobile who want a cheap "S&P 500 /
    whole world" ETF. The main barrier I expect is that their broker blocks US
    ETFs because those funds have no EU key information document (KID).
  - Concept: „Americké ETF vs. jejich evropská dvojčata". The page explains,
    with sources, why SPY and VOO are usually unavailable to EU retail investors
    under the PRIIPs rules. It then compares European (UCITS) funds that track
    the same indexes. It makes no profit promises and says clearly that it isn't
    investment advice.
  - Lead magnet: a PDF cheat sheet with the comparison table and a 7-point
    checklist for choosing an ETF in a broker app. The download appears right
    after the form is submitted.
  - Ads: Variant A leads with the problem ("your broker won't let you buy
    SPY?"). Variant B leads with the comparison ("4 ETFs available to Czechs in
    one overview").
  - Funnel tracking: page view → comparison seen → button click → form started →
    submitted → lead saved → PDF downloaded. UTM tags from the ad are saved in
    the browser session and sent as hidden form fields, so each lead shows which
    ad it came from.
  - Tech: plain HTML, CSS and a little JavaScript, with no build step. Hosting
    on Netlify's free tier, using its built-in form handling for storage and
    spam protection, plus a cookie-free analytics tool.

  What the research found
  - NYSE vs NYSE Arca: SPY and IVV are listed on NYSE Arca, not the main NYSE
    exchange (checked on the issuers' own pages, 2026-10-11). The main exchange
    listed its first ETF in 15 years only in November 2022 (PIMCO's BOND,
    according to an ICE press release).
  - The KID rule: the EU regulation (PRIIPs, Articles 5 and 13) requires a KID
    before a retail purchase. That US ETF issuers generally don't provide one
    comes from secondary sources, not official ones.

  Still unverified
  - VOO: Vanguard's page wouldn't load for me, so I have no VOO data yet.
  - UCITS fund figures: fees and start dates come from justETF and still need
    checking on the fund providers' own pages. Fund sizes have no date attached.
  - Czech brokers: I couldn't confirm whether specific Czech brokers allow or
    block these ETFs.
  - DIP (Czech long-term investment product): I only have secondary sources; the
    Finance Ministry's PDF couldn't be read.
  - BOND: one secondary summary lists it on NYSE Arca, which contradicts ICE's
    press release.

  Questions for you
  1. Is it OK to treat SPY, IVV and VOO, clearly labelled as NYSE Arca, as the
     "NYSE" ETFs? A strict main-NYSE-only set would be tiny and mostly bond or
     actively managed funds.
  2. Is Netlify acceptable for hosting? You'd need to connect the repo yourself.
  3. For analytics, do you prefer a cookie-free tool like GoatCounter, or Google
     Analytics 4 with a consent banner?
  4. Should the comparison cover all 4 UCITS funds (two S&P 500, MSCI World,
     FTSE All-World) or just the S&P 500 ones?
  5. Should the page mention DIP, briefly and with an official link, or leave it
     out?

  I'm stopping here until you approve.

  Sources: SSGA SPY, iShares IVV, ICE: first ETF on NYSE Big Board, EUR-Lex 
  PRIIPs 1286/2014, justETF on US-domiciled ETFs, justETF profiles for
  IE00B5BMR087, IE00BFMXXD54, IE00B4L5Y983 and IE00BK5BQT80, iShares CSPX UK,
  E15 / Generali survey, Forvis Mazars on DIP

       67 +### 4b. US-listed reference ETFs (context only, not "buy" cand
          +idates)                                                       
       68  
       68 -**EU retail access**: all of these are US-domiciled. Under PRI
          -IPs (Reg. (EU) 1286/2014), a KID must be drawn up by the manuf
          -acturer (Art. 5) and provided to retail investors before purch
          -ase (Art. 13), https://eur-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/1286/oj 
          -**[VERIFIED]**. In practice, US ETF issuers generally don't pu
          -blish EU KIDs, so EU brokers block retail buys while allowing 
          -holds and sells. Source: justETF (28 Mar 2018, UK-framed), htt
          -ps://www.justetf.com/en/news/etf/us-domiciled-etfs.html **[SEC
          -ONDARY]**.                                                    
       69 -- Whether **a specific Czech broker** blocks or allows these E
          -TFs, and whether "professional client" opt-ups apply, is **[UN
          -VERIFIED]**. The page must not claim that "nobody in CZ can bu
          -y them". The safe wording is: *"pro běžné retailové investory 
          -v EU obvykle nedostupné kvůli chybějícímu KID"* (usually unava
          -ilable to ordinary EU retail investors because of a missing KI
          -D).

          +nd the seller to provide it to retail investors before purchas
          +e (Art. 13) | **[PRIMARY]** | Reg. (EU) 1286/2014, https://eur
          +-lex.europa.eu/eli/reg/2014/1286/oj |                         
      117 +| US ETF issuers generally don't produce PRIIPs KIDs, so EU br
          +okers restrict retail purchases (holding and selling still all
          +owed) | **[SECONDARY]** | Interactive Brokers help page (via s
          +earch; direct fetch blocked, 403), https://ibkb.interactivebro
          +kers.com/node/2993; justETF (2018), https://www.justetf.com/en
          +/news/etf/us-domiciled-etfs.html |                            
      118 +| Exceptions exist: professional-client reclassification; some
          + brokers offer CFDs on US ETFs | **[SECONDARY]** | Same IBKR p
          +age |

          +VERIFIED]** | Not researched per broker |

          +ister not reachable via search; no Czech-language KID found |

       98 -## 6. Conversion funnel and attribution                       
      140 +## 6. Funnel and attribution (summary; details in `docs/implem
          +entation-plan.md`)                                            
      141  
      100 -**Funnel**: ad click (UTM) → `page_view` → `comparison_view` (
          -comparison table scrolled into view) → `cta_click` → `form_sta
          -rt` (first field focus) → `form_submit` → `lead_success` → `ma
          -gnet_download`.                                               
      142 +- **Funnel**: ad → landing (`page_view`) → `comparison_view` →
          + `cta_click` → `form_start` → `form_submit` → `lead_success` →
          + `magnet_download`.                                           
      143 +- **UTMs**: captured on landing, saved first-touch in `session
          +Storage`, and sent as hidden form fields with every lead. `utm
          +_content` also selects the matching hero headline.            
      144 +- **Spam and privacy**: Netlify honeypot and a time-to-submit 
          +field. Consent checkbox unchecked by default. Privacy page. Co
          +okieless analytics.                                           
      145  
      102 -**Form**: name (optional), e-mail (required), and a consent ch
          -eckbox that is **unchecked by default** and links to a privacy
          - note. GDPR: state the purpose, the retention period and the c
          -ontroller.                                                    
      146 +## 7. Tech and deployment                                     
      147  
      104 -**UTM attribution**: on landing, read `utm_source/medium/campa
          -ign/content/term` (+ `gclid`/`fbclid`), persist them in `sessi
          -onStorage` (first-touch), and send them as hidden form fields.
          - Each lead record then carries its ad variant.                
      148 +Static HTML, CSS and vanilla JS with no build step. Netlify Fo
          +rms stores leads. GoatCounter handles analytics. The owner cre
          +ates the Netlify and GoatCounter accounts.                    
      149  
      106 -**Spam protection**: a honeypot field and a minimum time-to-su
          -bmit check. The form host may add its own filtering on top.   
      150 +## 8. Remaining risks and uncertainties                       
      151  
      108 -**Resource access**: the thank-you state on the same page show
          -s a download button right away. The e-mail is not a gate on de
          -livery.                                                       
      152 +1. **Czech registration and Czech KIDs** of the UCITS funds: u
          +nverified. The page wording avoids the claim (§4d).           
      153 +2. **Broker-level availability**: unverified. No brokers are n
          +amed on the page.                                             
      154 +3. **The KID-restriction mechanism**: the US-issuer/broker-blo
          +cking mechanism has secondary sources only (IBKR help page, ju
          +stETF). The legal basis is primary. The wording uses "obvykle"
          +/"zpravidla" (usually/as a rule).                             
      155 +4. **Data freshness**: fund sizes and fees change. Every figur
          +e carries an as-of date and source link. The README notes that
          + data must be re-checked before any real campaign.            
      156 +5. **VWCE KID**: only secondary evidence that it exists. The p
          +age will link to Vanguard's product page rather than to a KID 
          +PDF.                                                          
      157 +6. **GoatCounter specifics**: custom-event API and free-tier t
          +erms come from my own knowledge **[ASSUMPTION]**. Verify while
          + implementing.                                                
      158 +7. **Czech copy**: needs a native-speaker read.               
      159  
      110 -## 7. Smallest practical tech and deployment                  
      160 +## Change log                                                 
      161  
      112 -- **Stack**: static HTML + CSS + a small vanilla JS file. No f
          -ramework and no build step. **[RECOMMENDATION]** Fastest to bu
          -ild, test and deploy, and easy to review.                     
      113 -- **Hosting + form storage**: Netlify free tier with **Netlify
          - Forms**. It stores submissions including the hidden UTM field
          -s and has a built-in honeypot, with no backend or API keys. Fa
          -llback: GitHub Pages + Formspree free tier.                   
      114 -- **Analytics**: one `track(event, props)` helper that pushes 
          -to `dataLayer`, plus one cookieless free tool such as GoatCoun
          -ter (supports custom events). Cookieless avoids a consent bann
          -er for analytics. **[ASSUMPTION]**: verify the tool's current 
          -free-tier terms. You, not me, would create any accounts.      
      115 -- **Lead magnet PDF**: written in Markdown/HTML and exported t
          -o PDF, with static hosting.

      117 -## 8. Risks and uncertainties

          - UCITS funds are not yet verified. Fund sizes are undated.

          -eality (needs your decision).                                 
      121 -3. **Regulatory wording**: we must not overstate. "Usually una
          -vailable" is accurate; "illegal" or "impossible" is not. No pe
          -rsonalised advice.                                            
      122 -4. **Czech broker availability**: unverified. Avoid naming bro
          -kers, or verify each one.                                     
      123 -5. **DIP details**: I only have secondary sources (48,000 CZK 
          -combined annual deduction limit; payout after more than 10 yea
          -rs and from age 60). The MF ČR PDF couldn't be parsed. Mention
          - DIP only briefly, with an official link, or drop it.         
      124 -6. **Free-tier limits** of the form and analytics services. Lo
          -w risk at demo scale.

  The two Xetra tickers (SXR8, EUNL) come from justETF only; the issuer
  factsheets don't show them.

  Corrections made to the report:
  - VOO was unverified before and is now verified.
  - Fund sizes now come from the issuers, each with its date. Before, they were
    justETF figures with no date.
  - VUAA's replication was corrected from "full" to "physical".
  - VWCE's fee: one source showed 0.19%; the current Vanguard factsheet shows
    0.14%, which is what the page will use.
  - CSPX's fund and share-class launch dates are now shown separately.
  - BOND and DIP are removed.

  EU retail access:
  - Primary source: the EU rule itself, that a KID must exist and be provided
    before a retail purchase (PRIIPs Articles 5 and 13).
  - Secondary sources only: that US issuers don't produce KIDs, so EU brokers
    block retail purchases. This rests on an Interactive Brokers help page (I
    couldn't open it directly) and justETF.
  - Still unverified:
    - Whether the four UCITS funds are registered for sale in the Czech
      Republic.
    - Whether Czech-language KIDs exist.
    - What individual Czech brokers allow.
    - VWCE's KID, which only an aggregator site shows.

  The report includes Czech page wording that says US ETFs are "usually"
  unavailable and asks readers to check with their broker. The page will make
  none of the unverified claims.

  Implementation plan

  - Files:
    - Pages: index.html, dekujeme.html (thank-you page for browsers without
      JavaScript), ochrana-udaju.html (privacy), tahak.html (source for the
      PDF).
    - Assets: assets/styles.css, assets/app.js, assets/tahak-etf.pdf.
    - Other: ads/ (two ad mock-ups), netlify.toml, README.md.
    - The comparison is written directly into the HTML, so it shows even without
      JavaScript, and each figure has its source link and date.
  - Form: handled by Netlify Forms with a hidden spam-trap field and a required
    consent checkbox.
    - With JavaScript, a successful submit shows an inline success panel with
      the PDF download.
    - Without JavaScript, it goes to the thank-you page, which has the same
      download.
    - The form also records how long the visitor took to submit, for filtering
      spam later rather than blocking it.
  - UTM attribution: UTM tags and ad click IDs from the ad link are saved for
    the browser session and copied into hidden form fields, so every lead in
    Netlify shows which ad it came from. The utm_content value also chooses
    headline A or B.
  - Analytics events: page_view, comparison_view, cta_click, form_start,
    form_submit, lead_success, form_error, magnet_download and source_click.
    They go through one helper, which also shows them on screen when the URL has
    ?debug=1. Each event name is tagged with the ad variant.
  - Deployment:
    a. You create the Netlify and GoatCounter accounts.
    b. You connect the repo to Netlify (no build step).
    c. You turn on form detection in Netlify.
    d. I add the GoatCounter script.
    e. We test production with both ad links.
  - Testing: the plan's checklist has 13 items. They cover screen widths, data
    matching the report, the disclaimer, headline switching, UTM fields reaching
    Netlify, the form with and without JavaScript, validation, the spam trap,
    Czech characters, events firing, the offline error path, Lighthouse scores
    and mobile browsers.
  - Order of work: the working page comes first, from the layout and verified
    comparison through the form, UTMs, analytics, PDF and deploy to the README.
    Ad mock-ups and ai-log/ come after that, and extras only if time remains.

  Before deploying I'll need from you:
  - The data controller's name, for the privacy page.
  - Your GoatCounter site code.
  - The connected Netlify site.

  I'm stopping here until you approve.

  Sources: SSGA SPY, iShares IVV, Vanguard VOO factsheet, iShares CSPX

  Follow these constraints:                                                     
                                                                                
  1. Work incrementally. Implement one coherent stage at a time, verify it, and 
  create a genuine Git commit after each working milestone. Never rewrite the   
  entire project unnecessarily.                                                 
  2. Start with the HTML structure, responsive CSS, and verified ETF comparison 
  data. Keep all numerical claims traceable to the research report and primary  
  sources. Do not add unverified claims.                                        
  3. Then implement the lead form, immediate PDF access, UTM attribution, and   
  analytics. Test each feature before moving on.                                
  4. Keep the solution static and dependency-free unless a concrete requirement 
  makes that impossible. Do not add unnecessary libraries or services.          
  5. Do not invent the data controller's identity, privacy details, or legal    
  wording. Implement a clearly marked draft privacy page, but do not enable     
  real public lead collection or claim legal readiness until the required       
  controller and privacy details are confirmed. Do not make marketing consent a 
  condition of receiving the PDF.                                               
  6. Preserve the distinction between S&P 500 funds and broader global funds.   
  Do not claim all funds are available to every Czech retail investor.          
  7. Create and maintain the README, two ad variants, and genuine Claude Code   
  session exports in ai-log/. Use /export for actual session exports; never     
  fabricate logs. Save relevant session exports as work progresses.             
  8. Run the available checks and manually verify the form, PDF download, UTM   
  attribution, responsive layout, and analytics. Report anything that could not 
  be tested honestly.                                                           
  9. Do not deploy a live form that collects real personal data until the       
  privacy details have been confirmed. You may prepare the deployment           
  configuration and explain the remaining steps.

  extra features until the required functionality works.

  How your constraints are handled:
  - No real lead collection: the form lacks Netlify's data-netlify attribute, so
    Netlify registers no form and stores nothing. A visible notice tells
    visitors it's a demo.
  - PDF without marketing consent: consent is an optional, unchecked box, and
    the PDF is given either way.
  - Privacy page: it's marked as a draft, and every detail I don't have is a
    [DOPLNIT] (fill in) placeholder. I wrote no legal wording.
  - S&P 500 vs global funds: they sit in separate groups, with a sentence saying
    they're not directly comparable.
  - Availability: the page says US ETFs are "usually" unavailable to EU retail
    investors and asks readers to check with their broker. It never claims every
    Czech investor can buy the UCITS funds.

  Checks run:
  - Tests: 19 browser tests, dependency-free, run with node tests/e2e.mjs. They
    cover:
    - the form in demo mode, in live mode against a local copy of Netlify's form
      endpoint, on server errors, and with the spam trap filled;
    - UTM persistence and the first-visit rule;
    - the order of funnel events;
    - every page loading under the production security policy.
  - Do the tests catch bugs? I deliberately broke the code twice and the tests
    caught both breaks.
  - Validation:
    - The HTML has one validator error, Netlify's own netlify-honeypot
      attribute, which is intentional. The CSS has none.
    - All 14 source links return HTTP 200.
    - The page has no sideways scrolling at 320–1280px.
    - Contrast meets WCAG AA in light and dark themes.
  - Visual review: I looked at screenshots of the page, both PDF pages and both
    ads.

  Not tested:
  - The real Netlify deployment and form storage. I only tested against a local
    stand-in.
  - Real GoatCounter data collection. I only tested against a fake.
  - Physical phones. Mobile was emulated in desktop Chrome.
  - Lighthouse, because running it from the command line needs a package
    install. You can run it from Chrome DevTools.
  - A native speaker's read of the Czech copy.

  Issues found and fixed along the way:
  - Anchor links would have scrolled under the sticky header.
  - Ad A had an unverified caption; it now just labels the phone image as an
    illustration.
  - The cheat sheet's inline styles would have been blocked by the security
    policy; they moved to a CSS file.

  Known limitation: with an ad link, the default headline may flash briefly
  before A or B replaces it. The README lists this.

❯ <pasted_content id="e3a7">                                                    
  I need to understand everything you built before I continue. Explain it to me 
  as to a junior developer, in simple English and short paragraphs:             
  1. List the files and folders and what each one does.                         
  2. Walk me step by step through what happens when a visitor arrives from an   
  ad, scrolls, fills in the form and downloads the PDF. Name the file

⏺ The project is a static website: plain HTML, CSS and one JavaScript file, with
  no framework and no build step. Below are the files, a visitor's path from ad
  to PDF, and where the ETF data lives. I haven't changed any code.

  1. Files and folders

  Pages (in the root folder)
  - index.html is the landing page. It holds all the visible content: the
    headline, the "why can't I buy SPY" explanation, the fund comparison, the
    form, the FAQ and the disclaimer.
  - dekujeme.html is the thank-you page with the PDF link. Visitors only reach
    it if JavaScript is off, or in demo mode through the <noscript> link.
  - ochrana-udaju.html is the draft privacy page. Anything you still have to
    fill in is marked [DOPLNIT].
  - tahak.html is the source of the PDF. It's a normal web page laid out like an
    A4 document, and we "print" it to PDF.
  - 404.html is the "page not found" page.

  assets/ (files the pages use)
  - styles.css is the design for all site pages: colours, light/dark mode,
    cards, form and the mobile layout.
  - app.js is the only JavaScript. It does four jobs: reads the ad parameters,
    swaps the headline, tracks events and handles the form.
  - tahak.css is the design of the PDF page only.
  - tahak-etf.pdf is the finished PDF that visitors download.

  Other folders
  - ads/ holds the two ads. Each one exists as HTML (ad-a.html, ad-b.html,
    styled by ads.css) and as a PNG image made from that HTML.
  - tests/e2e.mjs is the automated test. It starts a small local server, opens
    the pages in headless Chrome, clicks and types like a visitor, and checks
    the results.
  - docs/ holds the research report (step-1-research.md) and the implementation
    plan.
  - ai-log/ holds the Claude Code session exports. Your /export file is there
    now but not committed yet; tell me if you want it committed.

  Configuration
  - netlify.toml tells Netlify how to serve the site. There's no build step, and
    it adds security headers, including a strict Content Security Policy, a
    rule saying which scripts and styles the browser may load.
  - README.md explains the project, the decisions, the A/B ideas and how to
    deploy.

  2. A visitor's journey, step by step

  Step 1: The ad. The visitor sees ad A or B (ads/ad-a.png or ad-b.png) and taps
  it. The ad's link carries UTM parameters, for example
  ?utm_source=meta&utm_content=ad_a_problem. These tell us which ad the visitor
  came from.

  Step 2: The page loads. The browser downloads index.html, assets/styles.css
  and assets/app.js. On Netlify, the headers from netlify.toml are added here.
  The script tag has defer, so it runs after the HTML has been read.

  Step 3: Remembering the ad. app.js reads the UTM values from the address bar
  and saves them in sessionStorage, which is browser memory for one tab session.
  Only the first visit in the session is saved. If the visitor later comes back
  without parameters, the original ad is still remembered.

  Step 4: Matching the headline. app.js looks at utm_content. For ad_a_problem
  it swaps the headline to text A, for ad_b_compare to text B, and otherwise it
  keeps the default from index.html. This keeps the page saying the same thing
  as the ad.

  Step 5: Clicks and scrolling. Elements that should be counted carry a
  data-track attribute in index.html, such as the CTA buttons and the source
  links. One click listener in app.js catches these clicks and records an event
  like cta_click. When the first group of fund cards becomes about 30% visible,
  app.js records comparison_view. It uses an IntersectionObserver for this, a
  browser feature that reports when an element comes into view.

  Step 6: Sending events to analytics. Every event goes through one function in
  app.js, called track().
  - It names the event plus the ad variant, for example lead_success/a.
  - It stores the event in window.__events and passes it on to GoatCounter.
  - Right now the GoatCounter script tag in index.html is commented out, so
    events are only kept in the browser and nothing goes to an analytics
    service.
  - If you add ?debug=1 to the address, the events appear in a small box on the
    page.

  Step 7: Filling in the form. The first time the visitor clicks into the form,
  app.js records form_start. The form itself lives in index.html. It has an
  e-mail field, an optional name field, an optional marketing checkbox and some
  hidden fields.

  Step 8: Pressing submit. app.js stops the browser's normal submit and does its
  own checks:
  1. Is the hidden spam-trap field filled? Only bots fill it. If so, it shows
     success and sends nothing.
  2. Does the e-mail look valid? If not, it shows a Czech error message under
     the field.
  3. If everything is fine, it records form_submit. It then copies the saved UTM
     values, the headline variant, the landing URL and the referrer into the
     hidden fields, plus how long the visitor took to submit.

  Step 9: Sending the lead. The form has data-lead-mode="demo", so nothing
  leaves the browser; app.js just keeps the data in window.__lastLeadPayload for
  testing. In live mode, it would send the data to Netlify Forms with fetch. If
  that request fails, app.js records form_error, shows a message and keeps what
  the visitor typed.

  Step 10: Success. app.js records lead_success, hides the form and shows the
  success box. That box is already in index.html, just hidden until now, and it
  contains the "Stáhnout tahák" (download the cheat sheet) button.

  Step 11: Downloading the PDF. The button links to assets/tahak-etf.pdf.
  Clicking it records magnet_download, and the browser downloads the file. The
  PDF was made earlier from tahak.html and tahak.css using headless Chrome's
  "print to PDF". It's a fixed file; it isn't generated per visitor.

  Without JavaScript: in live mode the form posts directly to Netlify, which
  then shows dekujeme.html with the same PDF link. In demo mode, a <noscript>
  message links straight to dekujeme.html.

  3. Where the ETF data lives, and what was verified

  Where it's stored. There's no database and no data file. The numbers are typed
  directly into the HTML in three places:
  - index.html: the US table and the four fund cards.
  - tahak.html: the PDF table.
  - ads/ad-b.html: the four fees.

  So if a number changes, you must update all three and regenerate the PDF and
  the ad PNG. That's the downside of keeping it simple. The audit trail is
  docs/step-1-research.md §4. It records every value, its source URL, its as-of
  date and how sure we are of it.

  Checked on the issuers' own pages or documents (11 Oct 2026):

  Fund: SPY
  Source: State Street product page

  The fund sizes on the page are rounded to $0.1bn. The raw figures are in the
  report.

  Primary sources that aren't the issuers:
  - The KID rule comes from the EU regulation on EUR-Lex.
  - The MSCI World description comes from MSCI's own index page.
  - The 2022 note that NYSE's main board listed an ETF comes from ICE, which
    owns the NYSE.
  - The S&P 500 and FTSE All-World descriptions do come from the issuers
    (iShares and Vanguard).

  Not from issuers, and labelled that way:
  - The tickers SXR8 and EUNL come from justETF and carry a * on the page.
  - The explanation that brokers block US ETFs for retail clients comes from an
    Interactive Brokers help page (via search) and justETF.

  Still unverified:
  - Whether the four funds are registered for sale in the Czech Republic.
  - Whether Czech-language KIDs exist.
  - What individual Czech brokers allow.
  - Whether VWCE's KID exists; only an aggregator site showed it.

  The page avoids making any of these claims.

  │ same, then tap "Zobrazit      │ default    │ 0                          │
  │ srovnání"                     │            │                            │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ same, then tap "Stáhnout      │ default    │ cta_click/default,         │
  │ tahák zdarma (PDF)"           │            │ comparison_view/default    │
  ├───────────────────────────────┼────────────┼────────────────────────────┤
  │ same, then scroll to the fund │ default    │ comparison_view/default    │
  │  cards                        │            │                            │
  └───────────────────────────────┴────────────┴────────────────────────────┘

  The debug panel appearing at all already shows JavaScript runs, because the
  panel is created by assets/app.js.

  Issue 1: same headline for a and b

  Root cause: app.js (lines 44–56) only recognises two exact utm_content values:

  - ad_a_problem shows headline A („Broker vám nedovolil koupit SPY nebo VOO?…")
  - ad_b_compare shows headline B („S&P 500, nebo celý svět?…")

  Any other value, including a and b, falls back to the default headline. That's
  deliberate, so a mistyped ad link never shows the wrong promise. The short a
  and b you saw only appear in event names (cta_click/a); they aren't URL
  values.

  Watch out when re-testing in the same window. The first ad parameters of a
  browser session are kept. Once you've opened ?utm_content=a, switching to
  ?utm_content=ad_a_problem in the same window will still show the default
  headline. Use a new incognito window for each test URL.

  Issue 2: debug panel stays empty

  Root cause: the panel only lists events that track() in app.js records. Your
  page load and the button you clicked don't produce any.

  1. Page load adds no row. The page view is counted by GoatCounter's own
     script, not by track(), so it never appears in the panel. GoatCounter is
     also still switched off.
  2. Only elements with a data-track attribute record a click. In index.html
     those are:
     - the header link "Tahák zdarma";
     - the hero button "Stáhnout tahák zdarma (PDF)";
     - the source links;
     - the PDF download link.

     The second hero button, "Zobrazit srovnání", the FAQ items and the rest of
     the page have no data-track, so clicking them records nothing.
  3. "Zobrazit srovnání" doesn't trigger comparison_view either. It jumps to the
     top of the comparison section. comparison_view only fires when 30% of the
     first group of fund cards is on screen. Those cards sit below the US table,
     so after the jump only about 15% of them are visible on a phone and the
     event doesn't fire. I could reproduce this.
  4. form_start only fires once you click into the form.

  If you tap the hero's "Stáhnout tahák zdarma (PDF)" button, or scroll down to
  the fund cards, rows appear immediately.

  Gaps this exposed

  The code behaves as designed, but the design has blind spots:

  - "Zobrazit srovnání" is untracked. A visitor who taps it and lands on the
    comparison is never counted as having seen it, which undercounts the funnel.
  - The debug panel shows nothing on load, which looks broken.
  - Short values like a/b silently fall back to the default headline. That's
    safe, but easy to mistake for a bug.
  - The automated tests didn't catch any of this. They only use the full values
    and click the tracked buttons.

  If you want, I can track the "Zobrazit srovnání" click and make
  comparison_view trigger on the first fund card instead of 30% of the group. I
  can also show a "page loaded (variant: …)" line in the debug panel and add
  tests for these cases. I'd keep a/b unsupported and document the exact values
  instead. Tell me which of these to do.

      60 -- **Message match:** `utm_content` switches the hero headline t
         -o match the ad that was clicked (A: problem, B: comparison). Un

         +ly the exact values `ad_a_problem` and `ad_b_compare` are recog

         +y other value, or none, shows a neutral default.               
      61  - **CTA above the fold** on a 390px phone, repeated in the stic

      62  - **Short form:** e-mail (required) and first name (optional).

      63  - **Immediate delivery:** after submit the form is replaced by

          than a generic "invest in ETFs" page. Matching the headline to

⏺ All the fixes are committed as one separate commit, 637b21c, and all 28 tests
  pass.

  One change differs from what I proposed. Moving comparison_view to the first
  fund card alone wouldn't have fixed the "Zobrazit srovnání" case. I measured
  where the page lands after that jump: on a 390×844 phone the first card is 0%
  visible and the US table 100%. On a 360×640 phone it's 0% and 59%. So
  comparison_view now fires when either the US table or the first fund card is
  at least half visible.

  What changed, by file:

  - assets/app.js
    - The landing page records page_view/<variant> through track() as soon as it
      loads, so the funnel starts with page_view and comes from one source. The
      thank-you page doesn't record it.
    - comparison_view uses the new trigger above. It still fires only once.
    - The debug panel shows which variant was applied and why. For example, with
      utm_content=a it says "not recognised: use ad_a_problem or ad_b_compare".
    - The panel shows the URL value as plain text, so a crafted link can't
      inject code into the page.
  - index.html: the "Zobrazit srovnání" button now records compare_click.
  - assets/styles.css: one line styling the panel's new variant line.
  - README.md:
    - Exact values: a table of the utm_content values (ad_a_problem,
      ad_b_compare; anything else shows the default headline), with each value's
      headline and event suffix.
    - Ad URLs: both full ad URLs. The site isn't deployed yet, so they use
      YOUR-SITE.netlify.app as the domain, to be replaced.
    - Events and metric: the event table now includes page_view and
      compare_click, and lead rate is calculated as lead_success/<v> ÷
      page_view/<v>.
    - Debug and testing: a description of the debug panel, plus a testing tip.
      The first ad of a browser session is kept, so use a new incognito window
      for each test URL.
  - tests/e2e.mjs: 9 new tests, 28 in total. They cover:
    - page_view comes first and carries the variant;
    - "Zobrazit srovnání" fires compare_click and then comparison_view at three
      screen sizes, with no comparison_view on load;
    - a small peek doesn't count;
    - the debug panel's content, and that it can't be used for script injection;
    - a, b, A and ad_a all fall back to the default headline.

    Three older tests were updated to expect page_view first. Against the old
    code, 7 of the new or updated tests fail, as they should.

  A correction to my previous message: I said the old comparison_view check
  fired even with only 1px of the cards visible. In Chrome that's not true; it
  doesn't fire even at 47% visibility. I kept the more explicit check as a
  precaution and reworded the code comment. The two "small peek" tests confirm
  the intended behaviour, but they don't catch a real Chrome bug, because there
  wasn't one.

  To repeat your manual check, open /?utm_content=ad_a_problem&debug=1 in one
  incognito window and /?utm_content=ad_b_compare&debug=1 in another. The panel
  should show page_view/a or page_view/b right away, and "Zobrazit srovnání"
  should add compare_click and then comparison_view.

  The previous push questions still stand: your e-mail in the commits,
  docs/initial-prompt.md and the GitHub repo URL. Nothing has been pushed.

❯ We are starting Step 2 for ETF Guide for Czech Investors.                     
                                                                                
  This step is an audit and design proposal ONLY. Do not edit files, create     
  commits, install dependencies, deploy, or change Git history.                 
                                                                                
  Goals                                                                         
                                                                                
  Improve the mobile user experience and give the landing page a more           
  distinctive, professional visual identity suitable for a developer portfolio. 
                                                                                
  1. Audit the current experience                                               
                                                                                
  Inspect the existing HTML, CSS, JavaScript, ad variants and PDF where         
  relevant. Review the current implementation rather than assuming previous     
  observations are still accurate.                                              
                                                                                
  Focus on:                                                                     
  - Mobile layouts at 360px and 390px viewport widths.                          
  - The ETF comparison table, especially the clipped “Založen” column.          
  - Whether important information is readable without accidental clipping.      
  - Typography, spacing, colors, cards, buttons, borders, shadows and           
  decorative elements.                                                          
  - The visual hierarchy of the hero, ETF comparison, lead magnet, form and     
  sources.                                                                      
  - Accessibility, keyboard navigation, focus states and reduced-motion         
  behavior.                                                                     
  - Consistency between the main landing page and both ad variants.             
                                                                                
  Do not claim you tested a browser or viewport unless you actually did.        
                                                                                
  2. Propose a distinct design direction                                        
                                                                                
  The project should feel like a credible editorial financial guide for Czech   
  beginner investors, not a generic SaaS landing page or an obvious             
  AI-generated template.                                                        
                                                                                
  Consider:                                                                     
  - Strong typography and a clear hierarchy.                                    
  - Restrained colors and purposeful use of accents.                            
  - Clear presentation of ETF tickers, fees, indices, exchanges and sources.    
  - Minimal decoration that supports the content.                               
  - A clear path from the headline to the comparison and PDF download.          
                                                                                
  Keep the existing plain HTML/CSS/vanilla JS architecture. Do not introduce a  
  framework or dependencies.                                                    
                                                                                
  3. Mobile comparison                                                          
                                                                                
  Compare two approaches:                                                       
  A. A horizontally scrollable table with clear scroll affordance.              
  B. A desktop table and mobile fund cards that show the essential information  
  without horizontal scrolling.                                                 
                                                                                
  Recommend one approach based on the current markup, usability and             
  implementation complexity. Preserve all meaningful ETF data and source        
  references.                                                                   
                                                                                
  4. Deliver a proposal, not implementation                                     
                                                                                
  Return:                                                                       
  1. The most important issues, ranked by impact.                               
  2. A proposed visual direction with a small color palette, typography         
  approach, spacing and component principles.                                   
  3. A mobile comparison recommendation with pros and cons.                     
  4. A list of specific files and sections that would need changes.             
  5. A staged implementation plan with a small, reviewable first milestone.     
  6. How you would verify the result, including the existing automated tests    
  and real mobile viewport checks.                                              
                                                                                
  Be precise and refer to actual selectors, elements or files when possible.    
  Separate directly verified findings from assumptions.                         
                                                                                
  Stop after presenting the proposal and wait for my explicit approval. Do not  
  implement anything in this step.

⏺ Step 2 proposal: mobile UX and visual identity for ETF Guide for Czech 
  Investors

  The biggest problem is confirmed by measurement: on a 360px phone, half of the
  US comparison table is cut off with no sign that it scrolls. Below are the
  ranked issues, a proposed design direction and a first milestone that fixes
  only that table.

  What I actually tested: headless Chrome with mobile emulation at 360×800 and 
  390×844, light theme.
  - I measured how much of each table column is visible, which elements overflow
    the screen, real font sizes in pixels, touch-target sizes, the keyboard tab
    order and the heading order.
  - I took screenshots of the hero, comparison, form and FAQ/footer at 360px.

  Not tested in this pass: real phones, Safari, Firefox, screen readers, and
  dark mode (only checked in earlier sessions). Nothing in the repo changed, and
  the temporary screenshots are deleted.

  1. Issues, ranked by impact

  #: 1
  Issue: The US table is cut off on phones, with no hint that it scrolls.
  .table-scroll is 328px wide but the table is 640px (.data-table {  min-width:
   40rem }). At 360px, "Založen" (founded) is 28% visible, and "Velikost"
  (size) and "Zdroj" (source) are 0% visible. At 390px, "Založen" is 58%
  visible. The source links, the page's main trust signal, are off-screen.
  Evidence: Measured, screenshot
  Type: Verified
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Issue: The fund comparison is hard to scan on mobile. Each .card stacks 9
  label/value rows (dl.facts), and every value has the same weight (.facts  dd 
  { font-weight: 600 }). The tickers people recognise (CSPX, VUAA, SWDA, VWCE)
  come last, after the long official name. On a 360px screen the comparison
  section is about 4,400px tall (from y=1,262 to 5,661).
  Evidence: Measured, screenshot
  Type: Verified layout; scannability is my judgement
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Issue: The form is far down the page. #lead-form starts at y=5,935 at 360px,
  about 7.4 screens down (5,523 at 390px). The hero CTA jumps there, but
  someone who scrolls through the comparison gets no call to action until the
  form itself.
  Evidence: Measured
  Type: Verified
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Issue: The design looks generic. System sans-serif only, a standard SaaS blue
  (--accent: #0b5cad), 12px rounded bordered cards, a pill .tag, a yellow
  warning box and a plain-text logo. The ads don't match the page or each
  other: ad A is navy with a yellow accent that appears nowhere on the page,
  and ad B uses the page blue.
  Evidence: Screenshots, ads/ads.css
  Type: Verified; "generic" is my judgement
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Issue: Small text and small touch targets. .tag is 11.5px, .data-table small
  12.5px and table headers 12.8px. Source links (.card-src a) are 13.6px text
  in roughly 16px-tall targets (8–9 links per page), the "hvězdičkou" link is
  18px and .header-cta is 40px tall. Inline links have a WCAG exception, but
  tapping the sources is hard.
  Evidence: Measured
  Type: Verified sizes; the exception is my interpretation
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Issue: Weak hierarchy in the hero and notes. About 80px of empty space follows

  the hero disclaimer. The three important caveats sit in one dense yellow
  .notes box at 14.4px, styled like a warning.
  Evidence: Screenshot
  Type: Verified spacing; judgement on styling
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Issue: Accessibility basics are fine. The tab order is logical: skip link,
  header CTA, both hero buttons, source links, then the focusable table region
  (role="region" with aria-label). Every tested element shows a solid 3px focus
   outline. Reduced motion is respected for smooth scrolling, the only
  animation.
  Evidence: Measured
  Type: Verified, except screen readers
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Issue: The ads and PDF use a different identity from the page. tahak.css
  matches the page blue; ad A doesn't.
  Evidence: Code
  Type: Verified

  2. Proposed visual direction: "editorial guide"

  The idea is a calm, print-like financial guide: something closer to a well-set
  consumer finance article than a startup landing page.

  Typography
  - Headings in a serif using system fonts: "Iowan Old Style", Charter, Georgia,
    "Noto Serif", serif. There are no web fonts, and the strict CSP blocks
    Google Fonts anyway.
  - Body text stays in the system sans-serif.
  - Numbers (fees, dates, sizes) use font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums, so
    digits line up.
  - Tickers and ISINs use the monospace stack, with the ticker treated as a
    heading-level label.
  - Type scale: h1 about 30–34px on a 360px phone (currently 25.6px), body
    16–17px, nothing smaller than 13px.
  - I haven't checked Czech diacritics (č, ř, ů) in Iowan Old Style or Charter.
    Georgia and Noto Serif should handle them. If any font fails the check, a
    fallback is to self-host Source Serif 4 (an open-licence font, about 60–90
    KB); that's allowed by the CSP because fonts would come from the site
    itself.

  Palette (contrast to be checked with the existing script before use)

  ┌────────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬─────────┐
  │                  Role                  │        Light         │  Dark   │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Paper (background)                     │ #FAF8F3 warm         │ #14171A │
  │                                        │ off-white            │         │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Ink (text)                             │ #1B1F24              │ #E8E4DA │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Muted text                             │ #5B6470              │ #A7AFB8 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Rules (lines)                          │ #E3DED3              │ #2C3238 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Accent (links, primary button)         │ #1F5C4A deep green   │ #7CC4A8 │
  ├────────────────────────────────────────┼──────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Marker (asterisks, "exception" notes   │ #9A6B12 ochre        │ #D9A944 │
  │ only)                                  │                      │         │
  └────────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┴─────────┘

  Only one accent colour, and no colour coding of fund groups; groups are
  labelled with text.

  Spacing and components
  - An 8px spacing grid. Reading text limited to about 38rem wide.
  - Thin rule lines instead of boxed cards. Corner radius drops to 4–6px, with
    no shadows and no pill shapes.
  - Fund entries lead with the ticker, e.g. "CSPX · iShares Core S&P 500 UCITS".
    Then a prominent fee in large figures, then index and exchange, then a
    compact grid of the remaining facts, then the source links styled as clearly
    tappable "Zdroj →" (Source) links at least 44px tall.
  - Primary button: solid accent colour, square-ish corners. The secondary
    "Zobrazit srovnání" button becomes a text link with an arrow.
  - Hero: a kicker line "Průvodce pro české investory · ověřeno 11. 10. 2026"
    (guide for Czech investors, verified 11 Oct 2026), a serif headline, and a
    truthful at-a-glance line: "4 evropské ETF · S&P 500 i celý svět · zdroj u
    každého údaje" (4 European ETFs, S&P 500 and whole world, a source for every
    figure).
  - Notes: each caveat becomes a footnote marked in ochre, not a yellow warning
    box.
  - A short CTA after the comparison, before the FAQ, to address issue 3.
  - Ads and PDF share the palette and typography: ad A on paper and ink with the
    green accent instead of navy and yellow; ad B in the same style.

  3. Mobile comparison: A vs B

  The current markup is already a hybrid. The US funds use a <table> that gets
  clipped, while the UCITS funds already use mobile cards (.cards > .card > 
  dl.facts).

  What's visible at 360px
  A: horizontally scrollable table with a scroll cue: Still only about 3 of 6
  columns at once. Size and source stay off-screen until the user swipes.
  B: table on desktop, stacked rows/cards on mobile: All 6 fields visible with
  no
  sideways scrolling.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Effort
  A: horizontally scrollable table with a scroll cue: Small: an edge shadow, a
  "Posuňte →" (scroll →) hint and a sticky first column
  B: table on desktop, stacked rows/cards on mobile: Small to medium: add a
  data-label to each <td> and one CSS block below 48rem that turns rows into
  stacked blocks
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Data maintenance
  A: horizontally scrollable table with a scroll cue: One piece of markup
  B: table on desktop, stacked rows/cards on mobile: Still one piece of markup
  if
  done by restyling the same <table>, with no duplicated cards
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Accessibility
  A: horizontally scrollable table with a scroll cue: Table semantics stay
  intact
  B: table on desktop, stacked rows/cards on mobile: Restyling table elements
  with display: block can make some browsers, notably Safari, drop table
  semantics. Fix: add explicit  role="table"/"row"/"columnheader"/"cell". Needs
   a check in Safari, which is untested.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Fits the page
  A: horizontally scrollable table with a scroll cue: Mixes two patterns on
  mobile (scroll table and cards)
  B: table on desktop, stacked rows/cards on mobile: One mobile pattern for all
  seven funds
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  Risk
  A: horizontally scrollable table with a scroll cue: Users miss the hidden
  columns, especially the sources
  B: table on desktop, stacked rows/cards on mobile: Slightly more CSS; ARIA
  roles need testing

  Recommendation: B, by restyling the same <table>. It preserves every value and
  source link, removes the sideways scrolling that causes issue 1, matches the
  existing UCITS card pattern and avoids a fourth copy of the data. Desktop
  (48rem and wider) keeps the real table.

  Change: 7 new tests, and the page helper now exposes its DevTools session so a

  test can read Chrome's accessibility tree.

  How it works

  Below 48rem (768px), the same <table> is restyled:
  - Each fund row becomes a stacked block: ticker and fund name on top, then one
    line per field.
  - Each label comes from the cell's data-label, shown with CSS (td::before { 
    content: attr(data-label) }), so the data isn't duplicated.
  - The column header row is hidden visually but stays available to screen
    readers.
  - From 48rem up, nothing changes: it's the normal table, and the .table-scroll
    element and its class stay as they are.

  Test results (browser automation only)

  All of this ran in headless Chrome with mobile emulation; no real device was 
  tested.
  - Measurements and screenshots at 320, 360, 390, 768 and 1280px:
    - at 320–390px: no horizontal scrolling, no cell outside the table, no label
      overlapping its value;
    - all three source links fully visible;
    - at 768 and 1280px: the table layout as before.

    I looked at the 320px and 1280px screenshots myself.
  - Suite: 33/35 pass. The 7 new tests:
    - stacked layout with every field visible, at 320×640, 360×800 and 390×844
      (3 tests);
    - table layout kept at 768 and 1280px (2 tests);
    - every data-label matches its column header (1 test);
    - native table semantics in Chrome (1 test).

    Against the old CSS, the three mobile tests fail, so they would catch a
    regression.
  - Validators: the W3C HTML validator reports only the known netlify-honeypot
    attribute; the W3C CSS validator reports no errors.
  - Not tested: real phones, Safari or iOS Safari, Firefox, and any screen
    reader (VoiceOver or TalkBack).

  The analytics regression

  The stacked table is 862px tall on phones (previously 341px). After the
  "Zobrazit srovnání" jump, a phone mostly shows the comparison heading and
  intro text:

  ┌─────────────────┬────────────────┬──────────────────┬───────────────────┐
  │  Screen after   │  Whole table   │ First fund row   │ First UCITS card  │
  │    the jump     │    visible     │     visible      │      visible      │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ 390×844         │ 47 %           │ 100 %            │ 0 %               │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ 360×800         │ 39 %           │ 100 %            │ 0 %               │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ 360×640         │ 20 %           │ 61 %             │ 0 %               │
  ├─────────────────┼────────────────┼──────────────────┼───────────────────┤
  │ 320×640         │ 15 %           │ 44 %             │ 0 %               │
  └─────────────────┴────────────────┴──────────────────┴───────────────────┘

  comparison_view needs 50% of the table or of the first card, so it no longer
  fires on phones.

  Options:
  1. Recommended: in app.js, observe the first US fund row (#srovnani 
     .data-table tbody tr) instead of the whole .table-scroll, keeping the 50%
     rule. That's a one-selector change. It fixes 360×640, 360×800 and 390×844.
     On 320px phones it would fire only after a little more scrolling. Since the
     jump there shows mostly intro text, that's arguably correct, but it is a
     behaviour difference.
  2. Leave app.js alone and accept that mobile comparison_view fires later. The
     2 tests would have to change, and the funnel would undercount on phones.
     Not recommended.
  3. Point the hero button at the table instead of the section heading. That
     changes the hero, which is out of scope.

  Remaining accessibility concerns

  - Native semantics are kept in Chrome, so I added no ARIA roles. Chrome's
    accessibility tree at 360px shows the same table, row, columnheader (6),
    rowheader (3) and cell (15) roles as on desktop. Older WebKit versions
    (Safari) were known to drop table semantics when table elements are restyled
    like this. I couldn't test Safari, so that remains the main open risk. If a
    Safari or VoiceOver check shows lost semantics, the fallback is explicit
    ARIA roles on the table elements.
  - Labels may be announced twice. Chrome includes the CSS label in each cell's
    accessible name ("Burza NYSE Arca"), so a screen reader may say the column
    header and then the label. I left the labels exposed on purpose. CSS could
    hide them from screen readers, but then a browser that drops table semantics
    would read the values with no labels at all, which is worse.
  - Extra tab stop on phones. The .table-scroll wrapper keeps tabindex="0",
    which exists so keyboard users can scroll a wide table. On phones it no
    longer scrolls, so it's a tab stop that does nothing. Removing it means
    changing the HTML, so I've left it for your decision.
  - Already there before M1: the column headers are exposed in capitals ("FOND")
    because of CSS text-transform, and the table has no <caption>; only its
    wrapper region has a label.

  Which comparison_view option should I use? Once that's settled and the suite
  passes, I'll show you the final diff again before any commit.

  Remaining accessibility limitations

  1. Safari is untested. Chrome keeps the full table semantics in the stacked
     layout (6 column headers, 3 row headers, 15 cells), so I added no ARIA
     roles. Older WebKit versions were known to drop table semantics when table
     elements are restyled like this. If a VoiceOver check shows that, the
     fallback is explicit table roles.
  2. Labels may be announced twice. Chrome includes the visible CSS label in
     each cell's name ("Burza NYSE Arca"), so a screen reader may say the column
     header and then the label. I kept it that way on purpose: in a browser
     that drops table semantics, these labels would be the only way to know what
     a value means.
  3. A tab stop that does nothing on phones. .table-scroll keeps tabindex="0",
     which is only useful when the table scrolls sideways (desktop or wide
     tables).
  4. Already present before M1:
     - column headers are announced in capitals ("FOND") because of CSS
       text-transform;
     - the table has no <caption>; only its wrapper region has a label;
     - a 320px phone needs a short extra scroll before comparison_view fires.

  Verification

  All checks used headless Chrome on this Mac; no real devices.
  - Czech diacritics: Chrome reports a single font, with no fallback, for the
    full Czech test string in Iowan Old Style, Charter, Georgia, Times New Roman
    and the system sans, in regular and bold. On the actual page, the logo, h1,
    section headings and card titles render in Iowan Old Style and body text in
    the system sans.
  - Contrast: all 30 text and focus-ring pairs pass WCAG AA in both themes. The
    lowest is muted text on alternate paper at 5.23:1.
  - Screenshots: I reviewed 390px light and dark (hero, comparison, form) and
    1280px light and dark (hero, comparison).
  - Layout: no horizontal overflow at 360px.
  - Tests and validation: node tests/e2e.mjs gives 36/36, and the W3C CSS
    validator reports no errors.

  Limitations

  1. The comparison_view margin on small phones is thin. At 360×640, after
     tapping "Zobrazit srovnání", the first US row is now 52% visible (61%
     before M2), because the larger headings push it down. It still fires, but a
     later change in M3 or M4, or different fonts on Android, could tip it
     under 50%. A robust fix belongs in M4: point the hero button at the table,
     or shorten the intro text.
  2. Android and Windows are untested. Noto Serif, Android's usual serif, isn't
     installed here, so Czech coverage there is unverified. Windows would fall
     back to Georgia; I checked only the Mac version of Georgia.
  3. Form input borders have low contrast (1.3:1, previously about 1.35:1).
     WCAG's 3:1 guideline for input boundaries isn't met. I suggest fixing it in
     M4 with the form.
  4. The desktop hero gap (4rem plus 3.5rem) is unchanged.
  5. Ads and PDF still use the old blue and system fonts. That's planned for M5.
  6. The README doesn't describe the new tokens and fonts yet.

  I'm stopping here until you approve; nothing is committed.

❯ Before starting M3, please clarify how the light and dark themes currently    
  work.                                                                         
                                                                                
  1. Is the theme selected automatically through prefers-color-scheme, or is    
  there a manual theme switch in the UI?                                        
  2. If there is no manual switch, confirm that the site currently follows the  
  user's operating-system theme preference.                                     
  3. Please explain how I can preview both themes locally in Chrome.            
  4. Do not add a theme toggle or change any code yet. I only want to           
  understand the current behavior.

⏺ I'll check the code rather than answer from memory.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The theme is chosen automatically from your operating system's light/dark
  setting. There's no theme switch on the page. I checked the code, and nothing
  changed (the uncommitted M2 styles.css is still the only change).

  1. How it's selected. assets/styles.css sets the light colours on :root and
  replaces them inside @media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) (line 38). The pages
  carry <meta name="color-scheme" content="light dark">, so the browser's own
  scrollbars and form controls follow too. There's no data-theme attribute, no
  stored preference (localStorage) and no JavaScript checking the theme.

  2. So the site follows the operating-system preference.
  - In practice: with macOS set to Dark, Chrome reports prefers-color-scheme: 
    dark and the dark colours apply. Change the OS setting and an open page
    switches immediately, no reload needed.
  - Which pages: index.html, dekujeme.html, ochrana-udaju.html and 404.html
    share styles.css, so they all behave this way.
  - Light only: the PDF cheat sheet (tahak.html / tahak.css) and the two ads
    have no dark variant.

  3. Previewing both themes in Chrome without changing your OS setting.
  1. Serve the site: python3 -m http.server 8000 in the project folder, then
     open http://localhost:8000/.
  2. Open DevTools (Cmd+Option+I).
  3. Press Cmd+Shift+P, type "dark" and choose "Emulate CSS 
     prefers-color-scheme: dark". Use "…: light" to switch back.
     - Alternatively, open DevTools menu (⋮) → More tools → Rendering and set
       "Emulate CSS media feature prefers-color-scheme".
     - The emulation applies only to that tab, and only while DevTools is open.
  4. To check mobile as well, turn on the device toolbar (Cmd+Shift+M) and set
     the width to 360 or 390px.

  You can also switch macOS itself (System Settings → Appearance → Light/Dark),
  which affects every app.

  I haven't added a toggle or changed any code.

⏺ Found it: the beige band is a real artefact, visible after tapping "Zobrazit
  srovnání" or "Tahák zdarma".
  - Cause: section[id] { scroll-margin-top: 4.5rem } reserves 72px under the
    sticky header, but the header is only 53px tall.
  - Effect: a 19px strip of the previous section's paper colour shows between
    the header and the beige comparison section.
  - Fix: set the scroll margin to the header's real height.

  Plan

  1. Hero teaser.
     - A p.hero-teaser goes below the subheadline, with text per variant set in
       app.js alongside the headline. The default uses the A text.
     - A: „Místo SPY / VOO: CSPX nebo VUAA — stejný index S&P 500, roční náklady
       0,07 %." (Instead of SPY/VOO: CSPX or VUAA, same S&P 500 index, annual
       costs 0.07%.)
     - B: „S&P 500 (jen USA): CSPX a VUAA, 0,07 % ročně. Širší rozložení: SWDA
       0,20 %, VWCE 0,14 % ročně." (S&P 500, US only: CSPX and VUAA, 0.07% a
       year. Broader diversification: SWDA 0.20%, VWCE 0.14% a year.)
     - Under it, a small source line „Roční náklady podle správců fondů,
       ověřeno 11. 10. 2026 · zdroje" (annual costs according to the fund
       managers, verified 11 Oct 2026 · sources), linking to the comparison.
     - A test will check that the teaser numbers match the fund cards.
  2. Comparison order and disclosures.
     - The DOM order changes for all widths, so reading and focus order match
       what's on screen: section intro, then the S&P 500 cards, then the global
       cards, then <details> „Americké ETF pro srovnání" (the existing table
       inside, semantics unchanged), then the footnotes.
     - In each card, Sídlo fondu (domicile), Replikace (replication), Vznik 
       třídy (share-class launch) and Velikost třídy (share-class size) move
       into <details> „Podrobnosti" (details). Fee, index, ISIN, income type and
       tickers stay visible.
     - From 48rem up, app.js opens these disclosures, so desktop shows
       everything.
     - Copy fix: „stejně jako americké originály výše" ("like the US originals
       above") becomes „…americké ETF SPY, IVV a VOO", because the US funds now
       come after.
     - comparison_view now triggers on the first card's fee/index strip. That's
       more reliable on small phones, which also settles the M2 follow-up.
  3. Lead magnet.
     - Remove the first-name field, and update the tests, the privacy draft and
       the README to match.
     - Add a small preview image rendered from page 1 of the actual
       tahak-etf.pdf, placed next to the "what's inside" list, with Czech alt
       text and lazy loading.
     - The form stays in demo mode.
     - Input borders get a new --border-strong token with at least 3:1 contrast
       in both themes (the M2 follow-up).
  4. Spacing and typography.
     - The disclaimer, the last .steps li and the last FAQ details lose their
       trailing margins.
     - .section mobile padding goes from 2.5rem to 2rem, so colour-change gaps
       drop from 80px to 64px.
     - The scroll-margin fix above.
     - text-wrap: balance on h1–h4.

  What changed

  Hero
  - Teaser. A one-line answer to the ad's promise now sits under the
    subheadline:
    - A (and the default): „Místo SPY / VOO: CSPX nebo VUAA — stejný index S&P
      500, roční náklady 0,07 %".
    - B: „S&P 500 (jen USA): CSPX a VUAA, 0,07 % ročně. Širší rozložení: SWDA
      0,20 %, VWCE 0,14 % ročně".
  - Sources. The fees come from the issuers' factsheets, and a test checks each
    one against that fund's card. A source line links to the comparison. Variant
    B deliberately says "širší rozložení" (broader diversification), because
    MSCI World covers developed markets only, not "the whole world".
  - CTA position. The primary button stays on the first screen from 320×640 up,
    after a small h1 size reduction and tighter mobile hero spacing.

  Comparison
  - European funds first. The four fund cards now come before the US ETFs (SPY,
    IVV, VOO), which follow in a collapsible section „Americké ETF pro
    srovnání". The table inside works as before for screen readers.
  - „Podrobnosti" (details). On phones, each card folds domicile, replication,
    launch date and fund size into this section; fee, index, ISIN, income type
    and tickers stay visible.
  - Desktop. All of these sections open automatically from 768px, so desktop
    shows everything.
  - Data unchanged. All 36 card facts, 24 table cells, 23 links and the
    footnotes are identical to the previous version.
  - comparison_view now fires on the first fund's fee/index row, so tapping
    "Zobrazit srovnání" triggers it from 320×640 up (the earlier M2 follow-up).

  Lead magnet
  - The unused first-name field is gone: from the form, the draft privacy page,
    the README and the tests.
  - PDF preview. A small picture of page 1 of the real PDF (47 KB JPEG) now sits
    beside the "what's inside" list. It loads only when scrolled into view and
    has Czech alt text.
  - Input contrast. Input borders now have at least 3:1 contrast in both themes
    (the other M2 follow-up).

  Spacing and typography
  - Gaps. The large gaps came from trailing margins plus 2.5rem of section
    padding. I removed three trailing margins and set mobile section padding to
    2rem. Gaps between sections drop from 80–100px to 56–65px, and the page at
    375px is 1,526px shorter.
  - Beige band. It was real: anchor jumps reserved 72px under a 53px header,
    leaving a 19px strip of the previous section showing. The comparison now
    starts flush under the header.
  - Headings. Multi-line headings now break into balanced lines; I checked at
    320, 375 and 390px. One heading got worse (3 → 4 lines), so balancing is off
    for it.

  Tests and checks

  All of this ran in headless Chrome on this Mac; no real devices.
  - Suite: node tests/e2e.mjs gives 59/59. The new tests cover:
    - the teaser for each variant at 320×640 and 375×667;
    - comparison order and the collapsible sections, including opening them with
      Enter;
    - the removed name field and the preview;
    - the header gap and input contrast;
    - no horizontal overflow at 320, 375, 390, 768 and 1280px with every section
      open.

    Run against the M3 stylesheet, the preview, header-gap and contrast tests
    fail, so they catch what they target.
  - Tracking: every existing event still fires in the existing funnel and
    analytics tests.
  - Keyboard and motion: the new collapsible sections are reachable with Tab and
    open with Enter. No animations were added; smooth scrolling still respects
    reduced-motion settings.
  - Validators: W3C HTML shows only the known netlify-honeypot message; W3C CSS
    reports no errors.
  - Weight: the page weighs 95 KB in total, including the lazy-loaded preview,
    with no new dependencies.

  plus close-ups of the lead section and the first card for A):

  Limitations

  1. 320×568 (4-inch phones):
     - variant A's primary button ends 40px below the first screen (the sticky
       "Tahák zdarma" link stays visible);
     - after the "Zobrazit srovnání" jump the section intro fills the screen, so
       comparison_view fires only after a short scroll.
  2. Variant B headline. „S&P 500, nebo celý svět?" (S&P 500 or the whole
     world?) is existing copy, and MSCI World isn't literally "the whole world".
     The teaser avoids this; please decide whether to reword the headline.
  3. Without JavaScript, desktop shows the collapsible sections closed. Opening
     and closing them isn't tracked.
  4. Hiding the "+/−" and "→" symbols from screen readers is verified in Chrome
     only. Safari, Firefox, real devices and screen readers are still untested.
  5. Open follow-ups: documenting the design tokens and typography in the
     README, and aligning the ads and PDF with the new look in M5.

  I'll wait for your approval before starting M5.

      248  
      249  ## Known limitations
      250  
      251 +- **Collapsible sections and JavaScript:** „Podrobnosti" and „
          +Americké ETF pro srovnání" are native `<details>` elements. Th
          +ey open and close without JavaScript on every screen size, wit
          +h mouse, touch or keyboard. Only their automatic opening from 
          +768px (48rem) needs `assets/app.js`. Without JavaScript, deskt
          +op visitors see them collapsed and can open them with one clic
          +k.                                                            
      252  - **Headline variant swap:** the default headline can flash br
           iefly before `app.js` swaps in A/B, because the script is defe
           rred. Server-side or edge rendering would remove this.
      253  - **No e-mail delivery:** the PDF is downloaded on the page. L
           eads are only stored (once live), not e-mailed.
      254  - **The PDF is not truly gated:** it is a static file. This is
            acceptable for a free resource.

    and VWCE cover. The only numbers are the verified fees (0,07 %, 0,20 %, 0,14
    %) with their date.
  - No profit promises. Both texts end with the not-advice line, ad A also says
    to check availability with your broker, and there are no words about
    returns, gains or guarantees.
  - Lengths. Primary texts are 278 and 277 characters, headlines 25 and 32,
    descriptions 22 and 23. The key point fits in the first ~125 characters,
    which Meta usually shows before "See more".
  - CTA choice. I chose "Learn more" rather than "Download", because the click
    leads to the comparison and the PDF requires an e-mail address.
  - Check in Ads Manager before launch: the exact Czech button label, Meta's
    current length guidance, and the rules for financial-services ads in the
    Czech Republic. I couldn't confirm any of these here.

  Unlike the image, landing page and PDF, this README copy isn't covered by the
  tests, so it has to be updated by hand if a fee changes.

  Should I commit it?

✻ Sautéed for 1m 12s · done 3:24 PM
